Apollo’s Yahoo Playbook, 5 Years After the $5B Deal

Sourcery with Molly O'Shea(76.6K)·56:27·6.4K views·2026-10-19
Jim LanzoneCEO, Yahoo Reed RaymanHead of Apollo
TL;DRJim Lanzone reflects on Yahoo’s 5-year post-acquisition journey under Apollo, detailing a turnaround that has already returned Apollo’s capital, restructured the conglomerate into GM-led brands, divested money-losing ad tech and media assets, and launched new AI search capabilities.
The Verizon Era Context: Verizon previously acquired AOL and Yahoo, merging them into "Oath" (later "Verizon Media") under a multi-billion dollar mandate to support Verizon's 5G growth rather than Yahoo's core mission. The Apollo Deal: In 2021, Apollo Global Management (led by...

TL;DR

Jim Lanzone reflects on Yahoo’s 5-year post-acquisition journey under Apollo, detailing a turnaround that has already returned Apollo’s capital, restructured the conglomerate into GM-led brands, divested money-losing ad tech and media assets, and launched new AI search capabilities.

Acquisition & Deal Structure (2021)

  • The Verizon Era Context: Verizon previously acquired AOL and Yahoo, merging them into "Oath" (later "Verizon Media") under a multi-billion dollar mandate to support Verizon's 5G growth rather than Yahoo's core mission.
  • The Apollo Deal: In 2021, Apollo Global Management (led by Reed Rayman) acquired Yahoo and AOL from Verizon for roughly $4.8B to $5B (structured as ~$4.25B in cash, $750M in preferred interests, with Verizon retaining a 10% stake).
  • Capital Return Milestone: Five years post-acquisition, Yahoo has successfully returned Apollo's capital, making it one of the fastest returning deals in Apollo's history.

Reorganization & Operational Turnaround

  • Conglomerate Management: Unlike single-product companies (Pinterest, Snap, Reddit, Roku), Yahoo operates as a conglomerate of major independent brands (Yahoo Finance, Sports, Mail, News, Search).
  • Decentralized GMs: Lanzone restructured the company away from centralized cross-brand product/tech/content teams, appointing entrepreneurial General Managers with dedicated product, tech, content, sales, and marketing teams for each division.
  • Massive Restructuring & Layoffs: In February 2023, Yahoo executed a layoff of over 2,000 employees while completely overhauling its unprofitable ad tech stack.
  • Divestitures: Yahoo divested money-losing and non-core assets, including Edgecast (content delivery network), the native ad business (sold to Taboola for a 25% equity stake), TechCrunch, and AOL (divested in October of the prior year).
  • Ad Tech Optimization: Rather than feeding a proprietary, loss-making ad tech infrastructure, Yahoo shut down its supply-side platform (SSP) and optimized yield on Yahoo proper.

Audience Scale & Brand Transformation

  • Massive Reach: Yahoo reaches approximately 700 million users globally each month and ranks in the top 5 internet companies in the US, with 90% reach of the American online population.
  • Key Property Metrics:
    • Yahoo Sports: Over 100 million users/month.
    • Yahoo Finance: Over 100 million users/month.
    • Yahoo Mail: Used by one-third of all Americans (hundreds of millions of accounts).
    • News: Top 3 news aggregator in the US.
  • Direct Traffic Moat: Approximately 75% of Yahoo's traffic comes directly to the platform, and around 75% of users are logged in daily, heavily outperforming SEO-dependent publishers.
  • Gen Z and Millennial Growth: Over 50% of Yahoo's current audience is Gen Z and Millennial, with 75% of Gen Z utilizing at least one Yahoo product. Creative marketing campaigns (e.g., Cardi B, Kyle Cook weather boy) drove a 31% absolute point lift in Gen Z brand love.

AI Strategy & Yahoo Scout

  • Avoiding LLM Traps: Unlike companies building frontier large language models, Yahoo built Yahoo Scout—a proprietary AI answer engine—by leveraging its 30 years of search history, massive user data, publisher partnerships, and knowledge graph.
  • Scout Architecture: Scout grounds Yahoo's proprietary data and feeds it into lightweight models (such as Anthropic's Claude 3 Haiku at launch) to render unique, real-time results tailored to personal life management.
  • Reclaiming Search: Yahoo Scout marks Yahoo's return to having its own search engine for the first time in 17 years, reducing its historical vulnerability of outsourcing search to Microsoft.

Leadership Lore & Career Turnarounds

  • Jim Lanzone's Turnaround Track Record:
    • Ask Jeeves: Joined after his '90s startup was acquired; 50xed the stock; sold to IAC/Barry Diller, serving 3 years as CEO.
    • CBS Interactive: Grew unique users from 70 million to 220 million across 30+ brands; launched CBS All Access (now Paramount+).
    • Tinder: Served as CEO during COVID-19 in 2020 before departing for Yahoo.
    • Yahoo: Serving as CEO since September 2021.
  • Mentorship & Working with Barry Diller: Lanzone credits IAC founder Barry Diller and former Ask Jeeves CEO Steve Burkowitz as major influences, noting Diller's bold, original thinking during the IAC era.

Notable Quotes

*"We are a 5-year-old company managing a 30-year-old brand at this point."* — Jim Lanzone
*"You can't skip levels of the video game... You have to go level by level and win each level to earn the right to the next one. It always comes down to product."* — Jim Lanzone

Big Short Reunion w/ Danny Moses: Rising Rates, AI & Private Equity | The Real Eisman Playbook Ep 80

Steve Eisman(225.0K)·39:35·143.8K views·2026-10-19
Steve EismanHost, The Real Eisman Playbook Danny MosesHost, On The Tape
TL;DRFormer "Big Short" partners Steve Eisman and Danny Moses discuss current market risks, drawing parallels between today's massive AI debt issuance/securitization and the 2008 subprime mortgage warehouse lines, while also analyzing the federal deficit, the housing market, and private equity's growing monetization challenges.
Higher Rates for Longer: Both speakers agree that underlying interest rates will remain elevated over a secular cycle rather than a cyclical one. Treasury Limits: Danny Moses notes that government efforts to control the long end of the curve (such as Treasury bond-swapping) are...

TL;DR

Former "Big Short" partners Steve Eisman and Danny Moses discuss current market risks, drawing parallels between today's massive AI debt issuance/securitization and the 2008 subprime mortgage warehouse lines, while also analyzing the federal deficit, the housing market, and private equity's growing monetization challenges.

Fed Policy, Treasury Management & The Housing Market

  • Higher Rates for Longer: Both speakers agree that underlying interest rates will remain elevated over a secular cycle rather than a cyclical one.
  • Treasury Limits: Danny Moses notes that government efforts to control the long end of the curve (such as Treasury bond-swapping) are largely symbolic, stating that "nobody's bigger than the market itself."
  • The Housing Squeeze: Steve Eisman highlights mortgage rates sitting at approximately 7.5%, effectively stalling home purchases for average buyers.
  • Wealth Shift: Danny Moses points out a major structural shift where household wealth tied to the stock market now exceeds real estate, making equity market downturns a greater economic risk than housing.

The AI Trade, CapEx & Concentration Risk

  • Valuation Parallels: Danny Moses compares the AI craze to the 1999–2000 telecom and fiber-optic bubble, noting that while the technology is real, the sheer scale of CapEx, token economics, and cash burn by players like OpenAI and Anthropic requires strict mathematical scrutiny.
  • Balance Sheet Liabilities: Danny Moses notes that AI infrastructure provider CoreWeave carries $51 billion in debt (including leases).
  • Nvidia Concentration: Steve Eisman expresses concern over extreme concentration risk, citing Nvidia's recent 10-Q filing showing that 70% of its accounts receivable come from just five customers.
  • Securitization Parallels: Danny Moses draws a direct parallel between today's Wall Street-Nvidia securitization partnerships and the 2008 subprime era, pointing out how Wall Street warehouse lines initially fueled the mortgage boom before being abruptly pulled.

Private Equity and Private Credit Pressures

  • Monetization Lag: Steve Eisman highlights that the typical private equity monetization timeline has extended from 3-4 years to 7 (and potentially 10) years, creating cash flow friction.
  • The AUM Machine: Danny Moses explains that private equity built its massive footprint as an AUM-gathering game to replace traditional bank lending post-GFC, but the model has not been severely tested by a prolonged shutdown cycle outside of COVID.
  • Retail Stuffed: Danny Moses warns retail investors to be wary of private market products and gated funds being pushed into their inboxes, noting that institutional investors pass on these deals when the terms fail to favor them.

The U.S. Debt, Deficit & Global Reserve Status

  • Political Paralysis: Both speakers agree that neither political party shows any appetite to make the painful spending cuts required to address the growing national deficit.
  • Global Standing: Steve Eisman acknowledges similarities between U.S. debt-to-GDP and France's struggles, but argues that the U.S. remains insulated because Treasuries serve as the undisputed foundation of the global financial system.
  • De-Dollarization Risks: Danny Moses counters that the U.S. is "playing with fire," noting that the dollar's share of global reserves has dropped from 71% to 57% over the past two decades, while global central banks now hold more gold (27%) than U.S. Treasuries (22%).

Notable Quotes

*"Nobody's bigger than the market itself. Doesn't matter if it's the Fed trying to control the short end or if Treasury trying to control the long end. It's too big of a market."* — Danny Moses
*"Wall Street's willingness to lend to the mortgage originators to provide lines of credit so the mortgage companies could produce these mortgages... [is] the exact same thing happening with AI securitization today."* — Danny Moses
*"Nvidia is the largest company in the world, and 70% of its revenue comes from five customers. There's a tremendous amount of concentration risk."* — Steve Eisman

Buy, Build, Deploy: Using M&A to Drive AI Transformation — Prathik Naidu, Long Lake

AI Engineer(807.0K)·18:51·8.9K views·2026-10-19
Prathik NaiduFounding Partner, Long Lake
TL;DRLong Lake is an applied AI holding company that acquires established service businesses and transforms them from the inside out with AI, turning traditional high-labor service economics into software-like operating leverage.
The Outside-In Friction: Selling AI agents as a third-party vendor requires navigating enterprise security reviews, internal coordination, integrations with 20-year-old systems, user training, and complicated ROI/pricing models (tokens vs. seats). Organizational Complexity:...

TL;DR

Long Lake is an applied AI holding company that acquires established service businesses and transforms them from the inside out with AI, turning traditional high-labor service economics into software-like operating leverage.

The Challenges of Deploying AI as an Outside Vendor

  • The Outside-In Friction: Selling AI agents as a third-party vendor requires navigating enterprise security reviews, internal coordination, integrations with 20-year-old systems, user training, and complicated ROI/pricing models (tokens vs. seats).
  • Organizational Complexity: Real organizations rely on entrenched webs of team members with decades of experience, making it difficult for an outside vendor to understand tacit knowledge, edge cases, and human judgment.
  • Asymmetric Value Capture: An AI vendor might secure a 7-figure contract while generating $5 million in EBITDA impact (and $50 million in enterprise value) for the client, leaving the vendor struggling to capture the enduring value they created.

The Limitations of Building New AI-First Service Firms

  • Distribution & Brand Hurdles: Building an AI-first service firm from scratch means starting with zero customers, no brand reputation, and difficulty winning high-stakes enterprise trust (e.g., healthcare systems choosing a 20-year trusted architecture partner over an AI startup).
  • Expertise Acquisition: Hiring deep domain expertise in niche industries where clients require multiple specialty offerings is exceptionally difficult for new startups.

The Long Lake Ownership Model

  • The Five Pillars of Ownership: Acquisition provides structural advantages across Access (sitting directly with workers to design full-automation agents), Data (live environments with real edge cases and outcome signals), Incentive Alignment (shared P&L goals), Trust (decades-old brand reputation), and Scale (existing customer base, expert teams, and cash flow).
  • The Compounding Flywheel: Combining agents and people in live operations creates a self-reinforcing loop where real-world data and outcomes continually compound.

Service Economics vs. Software Economics

  • The 80% Service Tax: In a traditional service business, for every $100 of incremental revenue, about $80 goes toward delivery, overhead, hiring, and management, leaving only $20 of gross profit and creating a major bottleneck for growth.
  • Software Operating Leverage: Traditional software companies retain about $80 of every $100 added in revenue due to low marginal costs of growth.
  • AI-Driven Transformation: Deploying AI reduces the cost of service while improving quality, decoupling labor costs from revenue scaling and shifting service economics to look much like software.

Target Criteria & Transformation Playbook

  • Target Business Characteristics: Long Lake focuses on mission-critical businesses featuring end-to-end outcome delivery, human judgment, and regulatory accountability (e.g., handling urgent residential leaks, complex tax decisions, or last-minute corporate travel disruptions).
  • The Deal Pipeline: Long Lake reviews roughly 5,000 deals a year while maintaining a highly disciplined filter.
  • The Transformation J-Curve: Initial AI deployment requires heavy tech investment and workflow capture; once adopted, it unlocks massive capacity and time savings, which are then channeled into growth, higher revenue per employee, and improved client NPS.
  • Operational Scale: Founded ~3 years ago, Long Lake has raised $7.5 billion in capital (backed by Elad Gil, General Catalyst, Alpha Wave, D1, and others), partnered with 40 businesses across residential services, architecture, HR, and corporate travel (including the $6.3 billion take-private partnership with Amex GBT), and operates with a team of 55 people.

Notable Quotes

"The best firms have spent decades earning their customers, building brand and trust, and building an incredible team to deliver great service to their clients." — Prathik Naidu
"Software is always sold tools to people doing the work. If you zoom out a little bit, this is a services economy. It's a $70 trillion global market. And the important insight here is that owning the outcomes of a service end-to-end is far more valuable." — Prathik Naidu
Hey everyone. My name is Pratik Naidu. I'm a founding partner at Long Lake and today I'm excited to talk a little bit more about why acquiring businesses is a unique strategy to deploy AI into the real world. So to start out very familiar experience to many of you. Let's say you're building an AI product and now you're trying to sell it into the enterprise. Well, where do you start? You found a pain. You built an agent for it and it works, but for one very narrow use case. It's a great starting point. But now you have to actually get it into production. And that might involve dealing with security review, internal coordination, integrations with 20-year-old systems. It's a lot of hard work and this is typically the first wall in order to deploy AI into an enterprise. But once you get past this, now you have to get the team to use it. So you typically will iterate on the product interface, go through trainings, get feedback from users. And there's a typical question that comes up, which is whose job is it to actually drive up that adoption? Is it yours as the AI vendor or is it the client that you're working with? And then let's say you actually go through all this and now you're trying to figure out how do I actually get revenue for the agent that I just deployed. You'll typically work through a few different questions. Do you sell tokens or seats, maybe a bit of both? How do you deal with price sensitivity from your client? And how do you actually then measure ROI? And the bigger picture question here is as an AI startup deploying into the enterprise, how do you capture the value that you create from your deployment? This all might sound very familiar to many of you. It certainly is to me and these walls of deployment reflect a pretty common challenge, which is you're building on the outside and that makes deploying AI really hard. And importantly, it's not the model. The models, as you know, are getting incredibly good. Intelligence is exponentially increasing. So, what makes this really hard? And I want to talk about a few different observations of why deploying AI into businesses is challenging. The first is that organizations are incredibly complex. On paper, your org chart might look something on the left. You know, you have your owner, you have teams, managers, and this is a standard team structure. But, the reality is organizations are far more nuanced. There are decisions that are entrenched in a web of team members, often times folks with decades of experience. They're interacting with the sales team, the operations team. The owner often times leans on them. And so, building an agent as a third-party vendor is difficult because you actually need to embed into the organization. The second reason I want to touch on is you can't deliver the full value of your deployment if you don't understand the nuances of how a business actually runs. So, a vendor might integrate with your systems of record like a CRM or a ticketing system, or even, you know, document repositories where your SOPs, contracts, or, you know, old email threads live. And this is really valuable source of information, very useful. But, the reality is businesses, especially in complex domains, run on human judgment. It's the exceptions, the edge cases, the tacit knowledge, the, you know, quick questions that get asked to the expert that really matters. And that's really what governs a lot of how a company runs. And that's the operating reality. You need to work with the people doing the work to understand what great work actually looks like. The third reason that I want to highlight is that even if you are able to deliver end-to-end value, it's pretty hard to capture value as an AI vendor. So, let's say you land a seven-figure million-dollar contract, which is pretty impressive. But, how do you think about the enduring business value of that contract? A lot of big questions to answer. Will customers keep renewing? Can you maintain pricing as models improve and as alternatives get cheaper? But, let's say as part of that million-dollar-a-year revenue engagement, your client actually gets a lot of value from it. In fact, maybe $5 million a year in EBITDA impact. And so, what's interesting about this is that with an established business, you have years of operating history, enduring client relationships, and a proven business model with real economics. And so, you're adding earnings to a business with value that you can already reason about. And so, you know, using a, you know, roughly conservative 10x multiple, you can actually generate for your client $50 million in enterprise value, which is really interesting. And so, there's this question here, which is, you know, as a vendor, you did all the work. You've deployed your agents, you went through all the walls of integrating with the systems, working with the team. But, the largest impact actually shows up in the P&L of the business that you actually worked with. And so, these are the typical walls and challenges of deploying AI outside in. So, why not just go build that firm and build that business yourself with AI at its core from day one? This is a very valid strategy, actually. You can improve the whole workflow, not just a narrow slice. You sell the work itself, not just one specific tool. And importantly, you own the outcomes. So, the P&L and the value that you're generating is yours. And many of these firms are going to be amazing. We think this is a great strategy. But, I also want to call out some of the challenges that come up with this approach. The first is you don't start with any customers. It's hard to get distribution. You don't have any minimal you don't have any a brand reputation, so that makes it challenging to to grow. And it's hard to hire expertise especially in you know niche niche industries and important industries where clients might need multiple specialty offerings. So take an example here. If you are the leader of a health system and you're planning a new surgery building, do you hand that project to an AI architecture startup or the firm that you've been working with for 20 years who knows the standards, the nuances of how you actually design your buildings, and you can trust them to deliver the full scope of your project. It's a very different kind of execution model. And so the important insight here is that the best firms have spent decades earning their customers, building brand and trust, and building an incredible team to deliver great service to their clients. So instead of being outside in and selling an AI agent to a company or trying to build from scratch, what if you could partner far more closely with incredible businesses that already exist through acquisition? And I want to talk a little bit more about why ownership is such an interesting strategy here. Starting with on the engineering side, which is access. The ability to have your team sit down with the people who do work, understand what they do, build those relationships, understand the key people, the veterans, the folks that have the 20 years of experience is super important to really designing agents that not just solve one workflow or one use case, but really tackle the full automation and service delivery opportunity for the entire business. The second is data. Every business you can think of as really a live environment with real tasks, real edge cases, and real answers on whether or not an agent actually got something right versus not. And this is a huge opportunity to use that information, use that signal to actually improve your agents and models. But importantly, on the operational side, incentive alignment is huge. Ownership solves for this. You only win when your team and your business wins. And there's no separate revenue goals, no distractions from other clients. It creates an incredible amount of alignment that enables you to uncover and solve a lot of the blockers to ultimately get to faster deployment and faster shared goals. Outcomes really matter here. The other piece is trust, which we talked about. And, you know, importantly, rather than coming outside-in or building from scratch, ownership has that trust element baked in from the start. You know, you have the brand that took decades to build with all of the customer relationships. And and naturally plays into this idea of scale. Starting with a large base where you have those customers, you have an expert team that can deliver an incredible service, and the cash flow to continue to invest and compound in growth and new products is very powerful. And the important piece here is these five characteristics aren't just independent benefits. They actually create something far more powerful, which is a compounding advantage where a combination of your agents and your people continue to act in live operations. You can understand how work is done, create real-world data that leads to real outcomes, and that learning all compounds together. And this flywheel is really interesting, and it comes with all the elements of ownership to you know, power this flywheel from from day one. And so, what's interesting here is that ownership can compound rapidly over time. So, we talked about why ownership is is really interesting. And the important insight here is that owning the outcomes of a service end-to-end is far more valuable. And that's what's exciting to us. It's a huge market opportunity. But there's an important observation here, which is under the hood, there's a challenge that services businesses typically face. Labor costs typically grow with revenue. What's very interesting here is that AI actually reduces the cost of service while also improving the quality of your service. Don't just stop at capacity and time savings. How do you actually ensure that the work you're doing is driving real business impact for the companies that you work with, and delivering a far better client experience for the service that you're providing? So, if you're excited about owning outcomes and partnering with incredible services businesses, feel free to reach out. we're excited about this thesis. We think there's a lot of opportunity to go continue to build in the services economy, and feel free to chat more if there's any questions. Thank you.

Dylan Field: Is AI Replacing Designers?, Design is the New Code & Democratizing Creativity

All-In Podcast(1.2M)·40:33·72.7K views·2026-10-19
Dylan FieldCEO, Figma
TL;DRDylan Field explains how Figma survived a blocked $20B Adobe merger, navigated regulatory uncertainty, and is thriving in the AI era by championing design as the new code.
The discussion also touches on human creativity, market dynamics, and the intersection of AI with software development. - Company Foundation: Started in August 2012 by CEO Dylan Field. - Core Mission: To empower the best teams in the world to design, create, and build the...

TL;DR

Dylan Field explains how Figma survived a blocked $20B Adobe merger, navigated regulatory uncertainty, and is thriving in the AI era by championing design as the new code. The discussion also touches on human creativity, market dynamics, and the intersection of AI with software development.

Figma's Origins & Scale

  • Company Foundation: Started in August 2012 by CEO Dylan Field.
  • Core Mission: To empower the best teams in the world to design, create, and build the world's best software.
  • Expanded Use Cases: Used far beyond traditional design for marketing, advertising, slides, whiteboarding, and diagramming.
  • Current Business Metrics: Accelerated growth with 48% year-over-year revenue growth following recent market challenges.

The Adobe Antitrust Battle & Independence

  • The $20B Deal: Agreed upon as a partnership where Figma would retain autonomy, but ultimately blocked after 16 months of regulatory review.
  • Regulatory Toll: Field highlights the destructive nature of lengthy regulatory reviews, citing the blocked Amazon-Roomba deal as an example that forced Roomba into bankruptcy and shifted market share to China.
  • The 16-Month All-Hands: The announcement that the deal was dead occurred on the first day of winter break; the predominant emotion was relief from ambiguity.
  • Employee Retention: Offered departing employees a few months' pay and an easy exit if the startup grind was no longer for them; only a small percentage took the offer.

AI, SaaS, and the Product Layer

  • Model vs. Product: Field cautions against mistaking model progress for product progress, emphasizing that deep user workflows and instrument-like feel take years to build.
  • The "SaaS Apocalypse" Myth: Software is not binary (good or bad); specialized companies that obsess over user experience (such as Cursor) continue to thrive.
  • Figma's AI Integrations: Features like "Weave" (a node-based system for connecting model outputs) allow creators to shape AI-generated content like clay.

Human Creativity and "Design is the New Code"

  • Lowering the Floor vs. Raising the Ceiling: AI lowers the production floor and democratizes creation, but the ceiling is raised exclusively by human creatives who are bold and willing to try things never done before.
  • Design is the New Code: As coding models make programming accessible to everyone, differentiation shifts to design, brand, marketing, point of view, and emotional resonance.
  • The Attention Economy: In a ruthless attention economy dominated by algorithmic feeds and AI-generated decks, creators must be brave, say non-consensus things, and offer unique points of view.

Notable Quotes

"As coding models get better... the value is rising in terms of differentiation to the top. That's design, that's brand, it's marketing point of view. It's being bold. Uh and it's like the emotional reaction you're able to elicit." — Dylan Field
"The ceiling has stayed the same. AI is not raising the ceiling. Humans are raising the ceiling though... when they choose to focus, invest their time, and to be bold and try to do something that no one else has ever done before, that's when we see the ceiling raised." — Dylan Field
"Everyone can be a maker. Everyone can go do bold things. It's about willpower and it's about curiosity." — Dylan Field

How Security Leaders Can Prepare for Super Intelligence

Microsoft Security(64.2K)·34:25·2026-10-19
Satya NadellaChairman and CEO, Microsoft Hayete GallotInterviewer, Microsoft
TL;DRSatya Nadella and Hayete Gallot discussed how security leaders must prepare for autonomous agentic systems and superintelligence, framing these advanced models as "insider risks" due to elusive alignment and model opaqueness.
The conversation emphasized shifting from traditional security approaches to enterprise-wide architectures—such as zero-trust control planes, behavioral runtime monitoring, and tamper-proof governance—to protect organizational judgment and maintain accountability. Failure of...

TL;DR

Satya Nadella and Hayete Gallot discussed how security leaders must prepare for autonomous agentic systems and superintelligence, framing these advanced models as "insider risks" due to elusive alignment and model opaqueness. The conversation emphasized shifting from traditional security approaches to enterprise-wide architectures—such as zero-trust control planes, behavioral runtime monitoring, and tamper-proof governance—to protect organizational judgment and maintain accountability.

The Superintelligence "Insider Risk" & Alignment Challenge

  • Failure of Alignment Through Scaling: Satya Nadella noted that while the 2019 bet on scaling laws proved correct (reaching Astra-class models), alignment and mechanistic understanding do not automatically emerge with scale.
  • Opaqueness and Deception: Satya Nadella highlighted that models are increasingly opaque—sometimes due to policy, but also because chain-of-thought (COT) reasoning can be unfaithful as models "fake it."
  • Superintelligence as an Insider Threat: Because alignment is elusive and models cannot be fully understood from within, organizations must treat deployed models as insider risks requiring active behavioral monitoring.
  • The Need for External Controls: Satya Nadella explained that organizations cannot rely on models to police themselves and must instead build control mechanisms outside the models, utilizing multiple models, transparency demands, and deterministic controls.

The Reverse Information Paradox & Protecting Tacit Knowledge

  • Value Leakage via Judgment: Satya Nadella introduced the "reverse information paradox," explaining that unlike past digital technologies, learning systems leak a firm's core value—its human judgment—every time a user provides signals like thumbs-up/thumbs-down or trains the model on internal workflows.
  • Protecting the Theory of the Firm: A company's true trade secret is the tacit knowledge created daily by human capital working with digital systems. Organizations must defend this knowledge rather than inadvertently outsourcing their thinking to model providers (drawing an analogy to the East India Company).
  • Ecosystem Balance: To remain politically stable, AI must foster a frontier ecosystem of distributed knowledge where firms of all sizes and sectors can thrive.

Enterprise Architecture and the Control Plane

  • Control and Assurance Infrastructure: Microsoft's strategic goal for tools like Perception and Agent 365 is to provide a tamper-proof governance and policy control plane (an assurance ERP) that uses causal reasoning and non-neural network symbolic logic.
  • Identity, Observability, and Sandboxing: Satya Nadella emphasized starting with traditional least-privilege identity and observability, then evolving into advanced runtime behavioral monitoring of agent trajectories, custom sandboxes, and operating system primitives (such as Windows' MXC).
  • Zero-Trust for Agents: Security leaders need a ground-up zero-trust architecture designed specifically to manage multi-agent swarms and autonomous systems that break down traditional information-sharing silos.

Ecosystem Priorities: Data Security & Vulnerability Management

  • Data Security and Copilot: Satya Nadella and Hayete Gallot discussed how AI makes Information Rights Management (IRM) and Microsoft Purview more useful through automatic labeling, real-time data walls, and managing data boundaries across specialized agents (e.g., finance vs. HR).
  • Vulnerability Exposure and MDASH: Hayete Gallot outlined Microsoft's evolution in vulnerability management, moving from flat exposure graphs to context-aware prioritization using MDASH, reachability analysis, and exploitability telemetry.
  • Shift-Left Security via GitHub: Hayete Gallot detailed efforts with Jay Parikh to embed tools like MDASH natively into GitHub, scanning pull requests (PRs) to catch vulnerabilities before code is submitted and bridging the gap between security practitioners and developers.

Liability, Insurance, and Industry Standards

  • Human Accountability: Satya Nadella confirmed that because legal frameworks do not ascribe liability to agents, organizations and people remain fully liable for autonomous actions, which forms the basis for how insurance companies price liability.
  • The Push for Standards: Satya Nadella urged CISOs to use their procurement power to demand inspection standards and transparent reasoning traces *before* waiting for regulation, noting that these systems are deployed and active today.

Notable Quotes

"My assumption is that alignment may be elusive, even while scaling works. And mechanistic understanding is also elusive. So here we are, with more capable models coming down the pike... essentially now have quote unquote superintelligence as an insider risk." — Satya Nadella
"The reason why we don't have alignment is because the models are trying to fake it. And then on top of that, we have model companies trying to protect their IP and faking it even more." — Satya Nadella
"Most people think, oh, it's my data is my asset. Really it's not your data, your asset, it's the judgment around your data... And this thing leaks." — Satya Nadella

It's one thing to look at the code you have today and saying, "Okay, I need to find vulnerability." Again, appreciate your time and I look forward to the feedback. Thank you.

Jamie Dimon on the Biggest Risks Facing the Global Economy | WSJ Leadership Institute

WSJ Podcasts(167.0K)·40:54·2026-10-19
Jamie DimonChairman and CEO, JPMorganChase Alan MurrayPresident, WSJ Leadership Institute
TL;DRJPMorganChase CEO Jamie Dimon discusses the bank’s Security and Resiliency Initiative (SRI), global security risks like cyber warfare, and corporate leadership lessons learned during his two decades at the helm.
Origins and Purpose: Launched to bolster national security, advanced manufacturing, and defense supply chains across the US and allied nations. Investment Scale: Jamie Dimon notes the initiative will blow past its initial $1.5 trillion target, with $10 billion specifically...

TL;DR

JPMorganChase CEO Jamie Dimon discusses the bank’s Security and Resiliency Initiative (SRI), global security risks like cyber warfare, and corporate leadership lessons learned during his two decades at the helm.

JPMorgan's Security and Resiliency Initiative (SRI)

  • Origins and Purpose: Launched to bolster national security, advanced manufacturing, and defense supply chains across the US and allied nations.
  • Investment Scale: Jamie Dimon notes the initiative will blow past its initial $1.5 trillion target, with $10 billion specifically allocated for direct investing in security and resilience.
  • Scope and Ecosystems: Covers 29 industries (including grids, power, defense tech, space, and farming) and addresses complex supply chain vulnerabilities in areas like shipbuilding, semiconductors, and active pharmaceutical ingredients.
  • Advisory Board: Features leaders such as Jeff Bezos, Michael Dell, Condoleezza Rice, Bob Gates, and former general Paul Nakasone.

Geopolitical Risks and Economic Outlook

  • Top Global Threats: Jamie Dimon identifies escalating, unpredictable wars and cyber warfare as the biggest risks, calling cyber vulnerability "our biggest risk by far."
  • Supply Chain Vulnerabilities: Jamie Dimon criticizes past decisions to outsource critical items like rare-earth magnets to save trivial amounts of money, resulting in a loss of domestic manufacturing and IP.
  • US Economic Conditions: Jamie Dimon describes the US economy as currently booming with strong corporate profits, low unemployment, and low leverage among consumers and corporations, though he notes sticky inflation, massive government deficits, and risks from AI infrastructure spending.
  • Future Downturn: Jamie Dimon emphasizes that the economy will eventually face a cycle-driven downturn caused by a confluence of unpredictable events, for which JPMorgan stays heavily prepared.

AI: Adoption, Vulnerabilities, and Defense

  • Internal Use Cases: Jamie Dimon notes JPMorgan has utilized AI since 2012 across more than 1,000 use cases, including risk management, fraud detection, marketing design, and note-taking.
  • Broader Risks: Jamie Dimon warns that while major banks are well protected, critical infrastructure industries—such as water, transport, and smaller vendors—remain highly vulnerable to cyber threats amplified by AI.
  • Cross-Industry Collaboration: Jamie Dimon (chairing the Alliance for Critical Infrastructure alongside leaders like Chuck Robbins and Jane Fraser) highlights efforts by 50 major companies to build clearinghouses and vetting protocols for open-source code and security.

Corporate Leadership and Bureaucracy

  • Management Philosophy: Jamie Dimon stresses that a leader's job is not to make the CEO happy, but to do right by the company, clients, community, and country.
  • Avoiding Insecurity and BS: Jamie Dimon advocates for directness and intellectual honesty, warning against corporate bureaucracy, sycophancy, and avoiding "dead cats on the table."
  • Sports Analogies: Jamie Dimon draws management and teamwork inspiration from figures like Tom Brady, Peyton Manning, and the TV character Ted Lasso, emphasizing work ethic, protecting the front line, and creating a cohesive team.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • *None included in the transcript.*

And I think we can accomplish it with no new spending and no new taxes, just by changing immigration, education, permitting, all these various things you constantly read about. And we think it's funny—well, it took 12 years to get a permit to build a broken bridge. It's not funny. It's a disgrace. It's embarrassing to the country. It's one of the reasons the American population doesn't trust the effectiveness and competence of government. And so I think business can play a helpful hand by collaborating with them to fight for what's good for the country with policies that a lot of you already know and understand, because we have to deal with it all day long. And politicians—even the good ones—need help. We always—then the press, of course, when you see the press, it's often the people on the—I call them the wingnuts, but it's often the people on the sides. There are a lot of people in the middle, senators and congressfolks, who actually want to get the policy right. Fewer than there used to be. I think the press may give too much—I was so with Hakeem Jeffries recently when he said, "DSA is going to be five congressmen." Right. And that's all the time the press spends on it. And I just—tell the truth, the whole truth, and we'll see. Well, let's talk about any event Again, don't cry over spilled milk. Roll up your sleeves and try to help, because talking about it theoretically doesn't do a damn thing. Let's talk a little more about the economy. I mean, what is your analysis of where we are right now in the economy? Obviously, there are a lot of good things going on, but there's some concerns. You see it in the bond market, you see it other places. Yeah, so we already talked about the wars, and obviously that can dramatically affect the economy and a whole bunch of different stuff. So I'll talk about it non-the-war. I think that creates a huge amount of additional risk, and not completely controllable risk or understandable risk, because as I said, things happen you don't expect, particularly in wars. Look, the American economy is kind of booming right now. It's good times, you know, corporate profits way up. And the good news—and that's the thing, I don't like guessing about the economy. I like looking at the factors that drive it, and then you can add probabilities to it. Corporations are not over-leveraged. Consumers are not over-leveraged. Unemployment is really low. You can argue whether wages are going up enough for the lower end—probably not, but they have jobs, and that is the single sole most important thing for most people, that they have a job and they can go to work. And most people, contrary to what you hear, like their jobs. And what are the negatives? Huge government deficits, which the best way to reduce it is to have more growth. AI is adding to the growth because it's spending money. It's not—like the way it shows up in the economy is not an investment per se, because that's it's people being hired to build data centers and steel and servers and roads and cement, and that is kind of spending. So it may be a little inflationary at this point before it may have a deflationary effect. And inflation—it's been there for 5 years, folks. It's sticky. The numbers themselves aren't particularly good. So I think we should investigate how those numbers are calculated. And I think that some of these future inventions are inflationary. Ongoing deficits globally, remilitarization of the world, restructuring of trade, healthcare, immigration—proper immigration would actually, if we allowed merit-based proper-style more people working, seasonal workers, that actually would grow the economy and reduce inflation. So let's take that the next step then. You're a student of history, you look closely at it. When you see what's happening with inflation, when you see interest rates going up, when you see the level of investment that's going into data centers, we've seen this sort of thing boom before, and it usually is followed by a bust. I don't know. Look, when you read history, you're trying to make it a binary outcome, which what you guys always do in the press. Life isn't binary, and it might be it sorts itself out. I obviously it's reasonably concerning. Things are very good. Of course, one day we will have a cycle. So the notion—and I could give you all these reasons why it would be earlier, why it would be later—we will one day have a cycle. And then people attribute that cycle to their one thing or another. These people want you to put a date on it, if you don't mind. No, of course I can't. And the way JP Morgan looks at this is we analyze it to death, all these risks, and we can handle higher rates, lower rates, inflation, stagflation, equity markets down 40%, bond credit spreads gapping out two or three times, because we want to serve our clients in bad times. We aren't guessing about that. And if you and I sat—we all sat here, some of us think the probabilities of something bad happening in '27, some of you might think it's '26, some of you might think it's '28. Eventually there'll be a downturn. Eventually it'll be driven by a confluence of events that today you can't guess what they are. You can throw stuff on the wall and make believe you know. So we're prepared for all that so we can serve our companies, so we can serve our communities, so we can serve our country, so we can continue SRI. And those things will continue regardless of the economic environment. And where are you on the great debate over AI safety? The 10% chance that it's the end of humanity. Okay, let me be—I'd like to put my record here. I think the big picture is AI—we've been using it since 2012, okay? And we have a thousand use cases in risk, fraud, marketing, design, notetaking, hedging, customer errors, and I can go on and on and on, and I think it's the tip of the iceberg. I think it literally will over the years cure a lot of diseases, some cancers; fewer people will die on the roads; things will be taking care of you; you'll have more robots doing tasks that humans don't want. I think that's all true. And on the risk side, I think there are real vulnerabilities in AI, and you've seen them, folks. I don't repeat Hugging Face and this thing and that thing, and there are a lot of vulnerable units. So we spend a lot of money in cyber. Banks are rather well protected, as are other ones, but there are whole unprotected industries who are also critical: water, parts of transport, some tech companies, or smaller banks. We all have vendors, and one of the lessons when these guys go through vendors is that if those vendors don't have the people to double their production to help one of the prime contractors, or if they go down because of a cyber attack, either one could be bad. And yeah, we have human vulnerabilities. We have set up the—as I said before, Tom Fanning started the Alliance for Critical Infrastructure. Now chairing it. I'm chairing it, but that's only temp—I don't care who chairs it; I care about the effort. And Chuck Robbins is doing a lot of heavy lifting, Jane Fraser, and all that. Fifty big companies by industry, and we're already doing vetting and clearinghouses of open-source code, certain policy issues, and we have to disseminate that information. It's got to be done by industry at one point. And this is a new—we'll try to double down the effort. We have an extremely good board. It'll morph over time, and we'll be adding industries and companies, because I get a lot of calls: "Why not us?" Well, I got to start something. We had to start. So we're starting this way, but over time as we get more manpower and more capability, we're going to try to probably add industries and companies. And then I also think, take the banks. We should obviously work among ourselves, make sure our vulnerabilities cross-industry—like our vulnerabilities to telecom or water, our main vendors, think of Swift and Chips and Fedwire, and I can give you a thousand of those big ones, but also smaller ones. And then help our brethren, smaller banks and related, who have different vulnerabilities, different vendors. And that could be just training, all these various things. And a lot of things everyone should be doing today. Today. Network segmentation, identity access management, use the most powerful AI models you have to look for these vulnerabilities. It will find 20% of them. Learn how to patch very quickly. Make it part of your management meetings, so that if you're running this part of the company, you know the ones you need to fix. There are things people should start today to prepare for cyber. But it is really vulnerable, and I don't think anyone disputes that, by the way. You could dispute, again, on the scale of 1 to 10, is it 10, extinction, or 8 and a half? Where would you put it? I don't know. In my view it's It's rising. It was definitely rising, and it will change over time. So the efforts you're making here is to build the muscles to combat the bad guys. Remember, the bad guys could be states, terrorists, insiders who don't like your company, ransomware criminals. They all like to do different things, and so we need the muscle. And then maybe one day you talk to some of the big AI guys and LLM guys, they say we also are going to try to build models that are good defense. So if you have these thousand little agents—you know, they're little persistent agents that try to attack you now—you could build, maybe you'll have an AI system that offsets every agent that's trying to attack you, kills it. Well, we'll see. I want to talk about leadership because that's why the Wall Street Journal Leadership Institute exists, and that's why a lot of our CEO Council members are here. We've been talking about it, of course; everything you're doing in SRI is part of your approach to leadership. But you've been doing this 20 years now running this bank. Not many people make it for 20 years. I guess Warren Buffett did, Jensen Huang. To what do you attribute your success? How have you been able to do this? Yeah, I look, I don't really know, and I've seen a lot of very successful people do a lot of different things. To me, I'll tell you the important thing is you got to—I work hard, you know, and I'm relentless. People in this room know that. I just don't let go, and I love it. It's like—and I have the energy. When I don't have the energy, I shouldn't be doing it anymore. It has purpose for me. When I grew up, you had to—the values were work hard, have purpose. Everyone's got a different set of skills, or different sets of types of skills, and what's your—everyone's got it in different degrees. So it's a mix. You all have different mixes of skills. And I've seen people when they get a big job, their insecurity comes out. They start to cover up. You know, they don't embarrass the boss. The people who work for them is, you know, make the boss happy. The job of no one in this company is to make me happy. The job of this company is to do the right thing for the company, the clients, the community, the country. And I could see the difference when they talk to me. I'm not insistent on you making me happy. But here's the thing: they're putting people's analytical skills—but I think the real one for, like, leadership is the various EQs. And EQs are a funny one. Do people trust you? A lot of people aren't—people just don't like having them around. They're always looking around, who's saying what to who, how can they get away with it? Do you have grit to do the right thing when it's tough? Do you deal with a tough issue? Do you put the dead cats on the table? Do you have a little heart? People don't want to work for people who mistreat people. And I look—I've made that mistake where you promote the guy who's the best or lady who's the best trader, best banker, and they're a disaster, because they didn't have these other skills. And then some people get in these jobs and all of a sudden all these other skills come to light: how they manage people, how they take care of conflict situations. Everyone wants to call them up, and everyone wants them part of the team. And heart—I mean, when I first came to JP Morgan, they all had read the stories about me, tough on expenses, which I am. I hate waste. But waste is not tough on expenses; certain expenses are good expenses. But they had outsourced all the guards and janitors. And the head of the SEIU wanted to come see me to complain, I guess, about that. They didn't want me to do it. I saw him; they thought he'd be taping me and use it against me somehow. And he said, "You outsourced the janitors to the same person. He comes back to the same job at the same pay. The reason you guys did it is because the outsourced people took your benefits package—if they have a family, that cost $30,000, that's medical, predominantly medical and retirement—and they cut it in half. That saved $15,000. They let you have 7 and a half of that, and they took 7 and a half of the services provided." And I went to see the person who did it. I said, "Never do something like that again. We're not going to build JP Morgan a great company off the backs of our guards or janitors. That is not cost cutting." And I said, "I want them back by the end of the week. I don't want any negotiation. I don't want talk about it. I don't care what consultants told you. I don't care what other companies do." And you know, heart—those things matter to people. And so then you let people do their jobs, and it's a very—the other one is teamwork. People love sports. Sports is clarity. When you see a team and they're playing together—they don't have to be friends, by the way, but they play properly. Remember the Spanish team passing, taking their time, thinking about moving together. And you all know it. A basketball team or a football team, it's a beautiful thing to watch. Well, business is the same. It lacks two things. One is the clarity. BSers can BS endlessly. So you don't have the—did they win or lose? Now, we know that person is a BS artist, but we don't do anything about it. They're a good salesman or something like that. But it is the same thing when you see a company that works as a team. They support each other. They help each other. They give them a swift kick in the ass sometimes. Everyone does their part. Everyone does their homework. Everyone shows up. Everyone reads the tapes. And then you have leaders like Peyton Manning, and I always loved him, Peyton Manning, and Tom Brady. We have a conversation about leaders. They go through all this, and they both will say the same thing: "I didn't have a very good arm, and I couldn't run." Two of the greatest quarterbacks of all time, but they talk about the work ethic and caring about the front line. Tom Brady took less money so he could pay the front line more money. And there are things like that people don't focus on, but that's team. I think sports is—I think a big company or small, you could have the same bad behavior in a small company, too, by the way. Well, you're a Ted Lasso fan, too. Yeah, I love Ted Lasso. And what have you learned from Ted Lasso? He, first of all, it's funny, but he comes in and he doesn't know anything about football, and he's just gracious. But he's working with people, their insecurities, their bad behavior, their work ethic, and he's getting them to function as a team. And he's not arrogant, he's not—he always approaches things in a—not a humorous way, just an easy way to try to explain it. He's patient with people, but he wants to have a winning team. And it's just the psychology of how he's working that all the time to create that kind of atmosphere. And that's what I've seen in corporate America. Some terrible companies, I can't name them, who get a new CEO and they just go about fixing that, because most of these companies have huge capabilities and IP and great people, and a lot of big companies, they're political and bureaucratic, all that. But the people would like to get out of that, most of them, and some of the bad people want to keep it—they're like autocrats, they want to keep it suppressed because they benefit. And the good leader over time figures it out and removes some of the bad ones, and the place starts to shine. And it can take some time, because these bullshitters have been doing it for 25 years and they're usually pretty good at it. It's hard to notice in real time. And the reason you're so much fun to interview is because you say exactly what's on your mind, and I think it's only recently that I realized that's not just a personal trait, that's a management tactic on your part. You say what's on your mind, but you expect everybody else to do the same. Yeah. So if CEOs are saying stuff like how great they are, something like that, the whole company hears it, and they start playing to it. And companies know when you aren't doing a good job. And they would—and then so I say sometimes with this, "We're not doing this particularly well," because I want everyone to know. I'm not pointing fingers at people not doing a good job; I think we should all help them do a better job. But to BS about it, you will deny your problems and stuff like that. And so—just so you know, our people in PR and GR are exceptional, and they do take me through some of the stuff, and they want me to be direct and forthright, just not say it a certain way. Well, I was going to say, I mean, you're a big personality. They've got to be people who when you say something they just say, "Okay, yes, sir." Biggest mistake over the last 20 years? It's always the same nature. I mean, if I made a list, it's always people mistakes, where when I made that mistake, I tortured our people. And it takes a while to figure it out. The torture goes on for a while, then you got to replace them, and that is usually a big setback. You're a big reader. What's your favorite book that you've read recently? The one I mentioned is the one written by the granddaughter of Ike Eisenhower about how Ike led. NEC Director Kevin Hassett: Price increases are decelerating and wage increases are accelerating

CNBC Television(3.4M)·10:26·21.0K views·2026-10-19
Kevin HassettDirector, White House National Economic Council
TL;DRWhite House National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett defended the administration's economic record on CNBC's Squawk Box, pointing to strong GDP growth, accelerating real wages, and robust consumer spending, while dismissing recent inflation ticks as a temporary blip.
Top-Line CPI Numbers: - 12-month headline CPI shifted from a 3.5% expectation to 3.6%. - 12-month core change moved from 2.5% to 2.6%, described by Hassett as a "snoozer." - Near-Term Volatility: Acknowledged that recent monthly numbers show inflation needing work compared...
  • TL;DR: White House National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett defended the administration's economic record on CNBC's *Squawk Box*, pointing to strong GDP growth, accelerating real wages, and robust consumer spending, while dismissing recent inflation ticks as a temporary blip.

Inflation and Price Trends

  • Top-Line CPI Numbers:
    • 12-month headline CPI shifted from a 3.5% expectation to 3.6%.
    • 12-month core change moved from 2.5% to 2.6%, described by Hassett as a "snoozer."
  • Near-Term Volatility: Acknowledged that recent monthly numbers show inflation needing work compared to the prior three months, characterizing the recent reading as either a break from the downtrend or a simple "blip."
  • Positive Price Components: Noted that beef prices have dropped for four consecutive months and egg prices are also down.
  • Shelter and Discretionary Spending: Identified shelter costs—driven by spending on luxury resorts and hotels—as a major CPI driver, alongside airfares, reflecting high consumer demand supported by a ~4% GDP growth rate.

Wages and Economic Growth

  • Real Wage Gains:
    • According to estimates from Steve Moore's group using month-to-month Census data, real wages are growing at an annual clip of $2,000.
    • Average real American wage stands at $88,000, up $2,000 after adjusting for inflation since President Trump took office.
  • Economic Dynamism: Characterized the overall economic environment as a "boom," citing capital spending, accelerating wage increases, and decelerating price increases.
  • Unemployment Claims: Highlighted that the U.S. is experiencing the lowest unemployment insurance claims since World War II.

Consumer Sentiment vs. Actions

  • Partisan Polling Disconnect: Argued that economic surveys have "lost their value" in a polarized political climate, noting that Democratic survey respondents report conditions resembling the Great Depression while Republicans report favorable views.
  • Revealed Preference: Asserted that true consumer sentiment is reflected in behavior rather than polls—pointing to active spending on discretionary travel, fancy hotels, flights, and cars as evidence of a strong economy.
  • Historical Comparison: Noted that while real wages underperformed inflation for 2 or 3 straight months recently, cumulative real wages are up $2,000 relative to January, contrasting with a cumulative loss of $3,000 during the Biden administration.

Financial Markets, Housing, and Policy

  • The Federal Reserve: Stated that the White House respects Fed independence and trusts Fed Chairman Jerome Powell to evaluate data and make independent decisions.
  • Credit and Debt Monitoring: Revealed that the White House is closely studying credit default swaps and AI debt, noting it is being monitored intently with a detailed memo reviewed over the weekend, though it currently warrants attention rather than panic.
  • Mortgage Rates and Housing: Acknowledged that mortgage rates are up and "stubborn," but argued that incomes are also up and housing numbers showed improvement over the summer.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Data Confirmation Timeline: Kevin Hassett stated that high-frequency inflation data will be confirmed as either a temporary blip or a trend shift within *one month*.
  • Census Report Timing: Kevin Hassett projected that official Census report figures confirming annual real wage gains will be released in *about five months*.

Notable Quotes

  • > "This is either a sort of break from the downtrend or a blip. You know, it's the problem with looking at these high frequency data. We'll know for sure in a month." — Kevin Hassett
  • > "The economy is increasing wages. It's increasing prices to the price increases are decelerating, and the wage increases are accelerating, accelerating because of all the capital spending." — Kevin Hassett
  • > "If the economic polls that you're talking about were correct, then people wouldn't be taking trips to nice hotels. They wouldn't be flying, they wouldn't be buying cars, they wouldn't be doing all the things they do when they're feeling good about the economy." — Kevin Hassett

Secretary Marco Rubio: Americans Are "The Heirs" of Western Civilization

Breitbart News(415.0K)·30:44·2026-10-19
Marco RubioSecretary of State Matthew BoyleWashington Bureau Chief, Breitbart News
TL;DRSecretary of State Marco Rubio outlined a Trump administration foreign policy anchored in "America First" nationalism, the defense of Western civilization, a strategic shift toward the Western Hemisphere, and the dismantling of the Iranian regime's conventional and nuclear ambitions.
Cultural Heritage: Secretary of State Marco Rubio stated that the United States is an heir to the traditions of Greece and Rome, noting that the West is unique in being apologetic about its history. Global Receptivity: Rubio observed that while foreign leaders are privately...

TL;DR

Secretary of State Marco Rubio outlined a Trump administration foreign policy anchored in "America First" nationalism, the defense of Western civilization, a strategic shift toward the Western Hemisphere, and the dismantling of the Iranian regime's conventional and nuclear ambitions.

Defending Western Civilization and Foreign Policy

  • Cultural Heritage: Secretary of State Marco Rubio stated that the United States is an heir to the traditions of Greece and Rome, noting that the West is unique in being apologetic about its history.
      • Global Receptivity: Rubio observed that while foreign leaders are privately receptive to pro-Western messaging, they fear backlash from academia, media, and left-wing bureaucracies.
      • Electoral Shifts: Rubio predicted that the true shift in foreign policy will occur through elections as more leaders win office by embracing shared Western interests.

Energy Independence and European Geopolitics

  • The Greek Energy Hub: Rubio highlighted Greece as a vital partner for energy exploration and a key transit corridor for pipelines connecting Europe, Africa, and the Middle East.
      • European Energy Vulnerability: Rubio criticized past European reliance on Russian energy as the root cause of Putin's invasion of Ukraine, noting that reversing two decades of "green hyperbole" takes time.
      • Diplomatic Leadership: Addressing the dismissal of US Ambassador to Greece Kimberly Giloy, Rubio emphasized that Greece deserves an ambassador with the direct confidence of President Trump.

Western Hemisphere and USAID Reform

  • Pro-American Shift: Rubio pointed to a historic realignment in Latin America, noting that countries like Colombia and Peru have elected pro-American, anti-drug cartel leaders.
      • USAID Restructuring: Rubio stated that pulling foreign aid away from independent organizations like USAID has stopped the funding of anti-American NGOs disguised as environmental or indigenous groups.
      • Humanitarian Response: Rubio contrasted traditional foreign aid with the Trump administration's rapid humanitarian responses in Jamaica, Panama, and Venezuela, which he said directly demonstrate the benefits of friendship with the U.S.

Russia-Ukraine Conflict and Energy Markets

  • National Interest and Diesel: Rubio defended the Trump administration's 30-day moratorium on sanctions to allow Russian diesel into the global marketplace, citing U.S. economic needs.
  • Core Territorial Dispute: Rubio explained that the war centers on Donetsk, where Ukraine holds a critical 11% defensive line protecting Kyiv, while Russia demands full annexation.
  • Infrastructure Strikes: Rubio noted frustrations with both sides, citing Ukrainian strikes on commercial shipping and Russian ballistic missile attacks on civilian apartments in Kyiv.

Iran and the Middle East

  • Dismantling Iran: Rubio stated that President Trump achieved his objective of neutralizing Iran's conventional missile and drone factories to prevent it from hiding behind a conventional shield to build a nuclear weapon.
  • Straits of Hormuz: Rubio highlighted that 13 to 14 million barrels of oil are currently passing daily through the Straits of Hormuz under U.S. military escort, while crippling sanctions blockade Iranian oil exports.
  • Regional Instability: Rubio identified the clerical regime in Tehran as the root driver of instability, sponsoring proxy groups like Hezbollah, the Houthis, and militias in Iraq.

"America First" Doctrine

  • Core Principles: Rubio defined the "America First" foreign policy as a common-sense approach that places U.S. national interests above globalist institutions and international consensus.
  • Leveraging U.S. Power: Rubio emphasized that the U.S. will utilize its economic, military, and cultural power to secure American interests while simultaneously benefiting its allies.

Notable Quotes

"The only culture and the only civilization in the world today who seems apologetic about its history and its achievements is the west. And it's having a dramatic impact on foreign policy." — Secretary Marco Rubio
"For a long time we demanded countries be friendly to us but we didn't reward them. There was no like reward for being nice to the US... That has changed under President Trump." — Secretary Marco Rubio
"Our foreign policy, which is what I can speak about, has to be about what is good for America. I think every country should do that for their own countries." — Secretary Marco Rubio
All right. So, Mr. Secretary, really want to thank you for taking the time to do this with us here. We're here in the Thomas Jefferson room at the State Department. With regard to the war in Iran, I think people around the country ahead of this midterm election, they want to see this thing wrapped up. How do we end this thing? Is it similar to the—I think people want to see the wars over. How do we end this thing? But also, what have we accomplished there so far? Well, it's important to remind people why this started in the first place. Iran wanted to build a conventional shield of tens and tens of thousands of missiles and drones that once they got to that point, they would be able to say to the world, guys, we're now going to build a nuclear weapon and there's nothing you can do about it, because we have so many drones and so many missiles that we will destroy the entire region. So, you can't touch us. We are immune. The president was not going to allow them to build a nuclear weapon hiding behind the conventional weapon shield that they were trying to achieve. So, he destroyed their navy. He destroyed their air force. He destroyed their factories that make the missiles. Now they still have them, but they can't make as many as they used to. Far less able to make the way they used to, because he's destroyed their factories for drones and missiles. They don't have an air force. They don't have a navy. And they've lost a lot of air defenses as well. The large portions of their country are deeply vulnerable to future strikes if necessary. So he's achieved that objective. They can no longer hide behind a conventional shield to build a nuclear weapon. The second objective is they chose to take the straits hostage. They would have done it anyway. Imagine if Iran had a nuclear weapon. At that point, they could say, and by the way, we own the Straits of Hormuz, and you guys can't do anything about it, because we won't just hit ships, we'll use a nuclear weapon against you if you try to do anything about it. They are trying to control the Straits of Hormuz. They lost control of the straits. Today, on average, over the last 10 days and certainly over the last period, about 13 to 14 million barrels of oil are going through the Straits of Hormuz out to the global market, and some days it's actually equivalent to what it was pre-conflict. And the US military is involved in escorting and defending these ships from these strikes. Iran is still shooting at these, but by and large, we've been able to get that flow going. And at the same time, because Iran continues to fire at these ships, we have imposed crippling additional sanctions that Scott Bessent's doing a great job on and a blockade. The only country in the world that cannot get a single barrel of oil through the Straits of Hormuz is Iran. They're the only ones, and we're inflicting tremendous pain on them. In addition, these sanctions are costing them money, which means that's money they don't have. Every dollar you deny them in sanctions is a dollar they don't have for Hezbollah, a dollar they don't have for Hamas, a dollar they don't have for the terrorist militias inside of Iraq, and a dollar they don't have for their own military or their own nuclear ambitions. So, I understand people's frustration because we all want it to be wrapped up. In some ways, it is, and right now we are basically in a Strait of Hormuz defense posture and doing an excellent job of crippling their economy in the process. Yeah. So, Mr. Secretary, another big thing is this is actually similar to the successes that we've seen around Latin America and the Western Hemisphere across the broader Middle East. I think when we look back on what President Trump has done both in his first term here and in the second term, what you guys have been able to do here at the State Department, the Middle East looks very different than it did before. There are still major conflicts and different things going on in different places, but the pathway to—I mean, look, peace in the Middle East used to be a bumper sticker slogan. It wasn't a serious thing, right? Like now it's a serious thing. We have a pathway to real peace in the Middle East and prosperity in the region, and that's because of many of the changes that President Trump has brought to the Middle East. Talk to us about that broader vision here, because I think when we look back on it, it's going to be a really historic thing. So look, I think traditionally the American political class thought that peace in the Middle East, the core central issue is the Palestinian question. It's an important issue. It's not irrelevant. I'm not saying it's irrelevant. But I think what President Trump has exposed is that the number one driver of instability and war and conflict and the number one obstacle to peace in the Middle East is the Islamic regime in Tehran. And I think the evidence bears it out in Lebanon. Why is Lebanon a country that's used as an attack point against Israel and other interests? Who sponsors Hezbollah? Hezbollah is 100% an agent of Iran. In Iraq, why does that country still have terrorist militias that operate from their territory and can launch missiles against Saudi Arabia, against Israel? Who sponsors them? Shia militias sponsored by Iran, which is the only country in the region that has launched not simply proxy attacks, but direct attacks, direct attacks against Saudi Arabia, the United Arab Emirates, Bahrain, Kuwait, Jordan—I'm probably leaving somebody out—who has launched the only country in the region that's done that. Iran. Direct attacks in addition to what their proxies have done, by the way, with the Houthis now in Saudi Arabia. Who is the number one sponsor of the Houthis? Iran. So if you look at all of the things that are going on in that region and all the flashpoints that exist there, they all track back to the Islamic Republic of Iran, to the radical clerics that run Iran from Tehran. They are the central problem in that region. If there was no Islamic clerics, these radical Shia clerics in Iran, if they didn't exist, if that wasn't there, none of these problems would exist. You may still have a Palestinian issue. I would argue that issue would be easier to deal with if Iran didn't exist. So, they're at the core of all of these problems. And I think correctly identifying who it is that's driving instability and war in the Middle East is important in order to solve it, and that issue has to be confronted. And I think what's happened over the last few months is it's revealed it for the world to see. Okay. The last thing I really want to ask you about here is with the president's address to the UN General Assembly, he talked about the use of American power to achieve the American objective. Can you lay out that America First foreign policy for us? What is that America First foreign policy? How do we do what the president talked about in that address to achieve America First objectives? How do we use that American power to do it? Well, first of all, and I know that people in the media—not this media, but other media—like to criticize the term America First as some sort of evil thing. Let me ask people how many candidates would be elected if they went out and told the American people, my job is to put America second or America third or America last. If they don't like America First, they should have the guts to go out there and tell people I am running for office on the belief that our foreign policy should be driven by America second or America third. No one ever does that, because I think at its core, everyone understands that our foreign policy, which is what I can speak about, has to be about what is good for America. I think every country should do that for their own countries, and foreign relations is how you untangle the differences. So I think the president's basically saying common sense, which is our foreign policy and what we do around the world, is going to be driven by what's good for America. Now, we would argue that what is good for America is probably also good for many of our allies as well. And in those instances in which what's good for us and what's good for someone we like are not exactly aligned, then we'll work on it with diplomacy and try to manage all of that. But in the end, America First is common sense. It's what it is. And I think what the president is saying is that we as a country with the largest economy in the world, the most powerful military in the world, the most influential culture in the world, we are going to use all the elements of American power to further the interests of the United States, which we believe also furthers the interest of our allies and the broader world. But America First, meaning we're going to always do what's right for America before anything else. We're going to put it before the UN. We're going to put it before the global commons, or whatever you want to call it, the global order that people worshiped around here for a very long time. We're not against any of those things. We're just not going to put them ahead of American interests anymore. That's what the president's doing. And it offends a lot of people in these think tanks and editorial writers, but if we had listened to them, I mean, at this point, America would be a middle power.

Secretary Marco Rubio Delivers Remarks at the American-Uzbek Business & Investment Council

U.S. Department of State(506.0K)·10:44·845 views·2026-10-19
